# First Letter.

->My Friend!<-

You long ago gave me the task of sending you the writings of the Order of the Illuminati <note>The Illuminati was a secret society founded in Bavaria in 1776 by Adam Weishaupt, dedicated to Enlightenment ideals and often viewed with suspicion by the state and church.</note> found during the house search of Zwack <note>Franz Xaver von Zwack (1755–1843) was a prominent member of the Illuminati; the 1786 raid on his home led to the discovery of the order’s internal documents, which were then published by the Bavarian government.</note> as soon as they appeared in print. Finally, they have appeared, and herewith I forward them to you. I have them in my possession, though I have not yet read through them with the proper attention, and I am eager to hear your opinion of them. Here, the matter is causing quite a sensation. Everyone is reading, defending, contradicting, raising questions, judging, and seeking in one way or another to educate themselves further on the matter. As for me, I am still holding back my opinion on this whole affair. I must first reflect on everything and compare the principles and objects with one another before I can say what I think of it. The whole thing seems to me like a flood, the devastations of which one cannot 

<summary>This opening letter serves as a cover note for the newly published internal documents of the Order of the Illuminati, which had been seized during a government raid. The author notes the intense public debate and controversy surrounding the disclosure but remains personally cautious in their judgment.</summary>
<keywords>Order of the Illuminati, Franz Xaver von Zwack, house search, public sensation, Enlightenment, secret societies, Bavarian government</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the metaphor from the previous page regarding a flood and the difficulty of assessing its damage.</meta>

see sooner, until the waters have receded, or like the site of a fire <note>The original German "Brandstatt" refers specifically to a plot of land where a building has burned down.</note> that one should not buy until the rubble has been cleared away, and one sees which walls have collapsed and which are still stable, and whether one can rely on the old vaults or not.

Perhaps I will be able to explain myself more precisely to you soon upon more mature reflection. I must confess that the entire incident regarding <term>Illuminatism</term> <note>The specific ideology and organized activity of the Illuminati; the author uses the term here with a sense of dread regarding its political implications.</note> makes me very sad. If we examine history, we will find that such factions have always caused internal disruptions in the state, which—depending on whether an ambitious and enterprising head was at the helm—resulted in the subsequent separation of the most useful members of the state, distrust of one another, and finally entire revolutions. I know that you have more insight, worldly knowledge, and experience in this than I do, and you could therefore clarify many things for me.

For the time being, a few passages in these writings have particularly struck me, precisely because I cannot yet see what meaning they actually have. Whether these are merely the sentiments of individual individuals, or whether

<summary>The author expresses deep concern that the Illuminati’s activities will lead to political instability and revolution, comparing the current uncertainty to assessing a building after a fire. He asks his correspondent, whom he considers more worldly, to help him interpret specific confusing passages in the seized documents.</summary>
<keywords>political instability, state disruption, factions, revolutions, historical precedents, Illuminatism, building metaphor</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's inquiry into the secret writings of the Illuminati, specifically questioning whether the Order's internal rules were merely personal opinions or intended as universal principles for society.</meta>

one sought to use them for the whole of society and make them effective—and then, what the Order might have intended as the ultimate purpose of all this. Among the passages of which I speak here is:

1. The question on page 88: whether the new recruit grants to society or the Order the **right of life and death** <note>original: "*jus vitae & necis*"; a legal concept from Roman law that granted a patriarch or sovereign absolute power over the lives of their subjects or family members.</note>, for what reasons, or why not? —

Did the Order then consider itself a lawful society within the State? —

Could the Order believe itself entitled to present such a question to its candidate? — Or

Did the Order truly believe it possessed the **right of life and death**, and only wanted to test the sentiments of its candidates regarding it? —

By what constitution did the Order receive this right over life and death? —

Was this meant to extend only to the members of the Order, or also to those outside of it? —

According to which laws, and how, was the guilty party to be processed, and how was the sentence to be executed <note>The original German "exequiert" refers to the formal carrying out of a judicial sentence.</note> upon him? —

...is understood no sooner than when the floodwaters have receded, or like a site of fire <note>original: "Brandstatt"; a plot of land where a building has burned down.</note>, which one should not buy until the rubble has been cleared away, to see which walls have collapsed and which are still stable, and whether one can rely on the old vaults or not.

Perhaps I will soon be able to explain myself more precisely to you upon more mature consideration. I must confess to you that the entire incident concerning Illuminatism <note>The term "Illuminatismus" refers to the movement and ideology of the Order of the Illuminati, founded in Bavaria in 1776.</note> makes me very sad. If we examine history, we will find that such factions <note>original: "Partheyen"; refers here to secret groups or political cliques.</note> have always caused internal disruptions in the State through which—depending on whether an ambitious and enterprising mind was at the helm—there followed the separation of the most useful members of the State <term>Staatsglieder: the constituent parts of a society, such as civil servants, scholars, and citizens, viewed as organs of a political body</term>, mutual mistrust, and finally entire revolutions. I know that you have more insight, worldliness, and experience in this than I, and you could therefore clarify much for me.

<page-num>4</page-num>

Has no use yet been made of this supposed right? —

But if the Order does not grant itself this right, why does it pose this question to its candidates <term>Candidaten: applicants or initiates seeking higher degrees within the secret society</term>? —

Why does the Order not also ask whether it has the right to block the Danube <note>The Danube was a vital economic artery for the Holy Roman Empire; the author uses this comparison to highlight the absurdity of a private group claiming powers that belong only to a sovereign state.</note> and to demand toll from the passing ships? —

Must this question not have awakened strange thoughts in the hearts of the candidates? —

2. The content of the *Major Cabala* <note>original: "Cabala Major"; likely referring to a specific section of the Illuminati's "secret scientific" writings or rituals found when the Bavarian government raided their archives.</note> described on pages 107, 108, 109, and 110, which contains the description of a burning-box <note>original: "Brennkiste"; a specialized security chest designed to incinerate sensitive documents if the lock was tampered with by outsiders.</note> for the safekeeping of secret papers, — the design of a secret lock, — means to excite uterine frenzy <note>original Latin: "ad excitandum furorem uterinum"; a historical medical term for extreme sexual arousal or what was then called "hysteria."</note> — means for inducing abortion — against toothache — recipes for *Aqua Tofana* <note>original: "aqua doffana"; a notorious, tasteless, and slow-acting poison famous in the 17th and 18th centuries for being undetectable.</note> — of poisonous plants — how to bring a poisonous scent into a room — of sympathetic ink <note>original: "sympatetischer Dinte"; invisible ink used for clandestine communications.</note>, and how to take impressions of seals <note>A technique for forging or intercepting private correspondence by making molds of wax seals.</note>. —

3. The expression on page 259: How is it going with the color experiments; have you not yet made an attempt? —

Should this be knowledge intended only for specific individuals, or did it serve the utility of the entire Order? —

If this knowledge belonged to the whole Order, was it connected to the supposed right over life and death? —

If it did not belong to the Order, why did it fall into foreign hands? —

Why are most of these writings written in Order ciphers <term>Ordenſchifern: secret codes and alphabets used by the Illuminati to ensure that intercepted documents remained unreadable to the uninitiated</term>? —

Since the making of seal impressions <term>Pettſchaften: signet seals used to close and authenticate letters; the ability to duplicate them was a primary tool for forgers and spies</term> is otherwise usually the occupation of forgers, why would this knowledge interest the illustrious Order? —

What important, or what dangerous papers must these have been, for whose safekeeping such ingenious means were sought? —

Of what use could it be to the Society to be able to promote uterine frenzy <note>original Latin: "furorem uterinum"; a historical medical diagnosis for extreme sexual arousal in women, then considered a form of madness</note> and abortion <note>original Latin: "Abortum"</note>?

To what end were the other assassin-like arts involving various types of poisoning? —

3. The expression of Marius <note>pseudonym for Baron von Costanzo, a high-ranking member of the Illuminati</note> to Cato <note>pseudonym for Adam Weishaupt, the founder of the Order</note> on page 387: "For my business went according to desire, and when you hear how one thing followed another, you will join me in admiring the secret springs of Fate <note>original Latin: "Fatums"; used here to suggest a hidden or providential hand guiding the Order's success</note>, but even more so in what I settled with *Alcibiades* in *Thebes* <note>the Illuminati code name for the city of Freising</note> during my passage through, which is so important that I cannot reveal it to you in writing at all." — — — Page 388: "Much luck to your undertakings: but reveal nothing to the Arch- <note>German: "Erz."; likely referring to the Archbishop or a high-ranking ecclesiastical official whom the Order was attempting to influence or deceive</note> before the time, if the matter is to bear fruit."

What are these secret springs of Fate? —

What was settled that was so important that it could not be revealed in writing at all? —

The entire business was, after all, initiated and directed through letters; why then was the aforementioned important matter forbidden from being revealed in writing? —

6. Page 183: a newly admitted member is to be assigned, among other things, the following question to work on: **"What advantages do such secret orders have over civil government?"** — — — Page 275: "If you continue as you have for some time, our fatherland will belong to us in a short while." — Page 221: "In five years, you shall be astonished by what we have done."

From this it seems evident that the secret order truly considered itself superior to the civil government.

If the Order conceived of itself as having this superiority, it must also have sought to exert a government-like influence over everything.

How should a society be viewed within a state when it considers itself superior to the government and acts according to a plan carefully devised for every eventuality? —

<meta>This page continues the critique of the secret order’s influence (likely the Illuminati), moving from their infiltration of the church to their broader ambitions regarding civil government and state authority. Can the civil government <term>bürgerliche Regierung: the secular, legal administration of the state</term> be granted to anyone other than those to whom it has been entrusted and handed over by the rulers <term>Regenten: sovereigns or governing monarchs</term> and the people? —

Who is the one who seeks to seize the rights of government for himself, whether in an indirect or direct manner? —

If one is to be astonished in five years at what they have accomplished; if, after diligent perseverance in their work, our fatherland <term>Vaterland: the ancestral homeland or nation</term> is to be in their hands in a short time, and if they hope for and desire this, while always demanding from their subordinates not mere words, but actual services <term>Realdienste: concrete, practical labor and duty as opposed to mere theoretical loyalty or rhetoric</term>, does this not explicitly prove that their aim was nothing less than the actual government of the state <term>Staat</term>, and to bring everything under their yoke and under their feet? —

After all members were guided in such a way that they had to act and work blindly according to the plans and principles <term>Grundsätze: the foundational rules and ideological maxims of the secret society</term> prescribed for them—these principles, however, as it has seemed to me, being somewhat ambiguous—what fruits would have resulted for the welfare of the state

...have occurred, if this society had gained the upper hand? —

Since they were all so closely bonded among themselves as brothers, and seemed to live only for their social bond <note>original Latin: "nexum socialem"; referring here to the internal, exclusive contract binding the members of the society together</note>, what fate would the remaining citizens of the state (who were not intertwined in their social bond <note>original Latin: "nexu sociali"</note>) have met with this society in power? — Would they too have been entitled to a positive happiness <term>Glückseligkeit: a central Enlightenment concept referring to the state's duty to provide for the prosperity and well-being of its citizens</term>? — Or would they only have been good enough to supply the others with oats and hay, or the beams for their buildings? <note>The author implies that non-members would be reduced to mere laborers or "beasts of burden" providing raw materials for the "architects" of the secret society.</note> —

How must a society, which intends to have the fatherland <term>Vaterland: the ancestral homeland or nation</term> in its power in a short time, view the Prince <term>Fürst: the sovereign ruler or monarch</term> and the national government? —

Can a healthy and punctual obedience to the laws of the land ever be expected from people who themselves strive for supreme authority <term>Oberherrschaft: total or sovereign control</term>? —

In all the countries of Europe, what punishment is ordained for one who seeks to seize sovereign power <term>oberherrsliche Gewalt: the legal authority held by a monarch or state</term> either indirectly or directly? —

# Second Letter.

## Answer to the Foregoing.

I have duly received the book sent to me by you, my friend, containing the **original writings of the Order of the Illuminati** <term>Originalschriften des Illuminatenordens: a reference to the secret documents of the Illuminati seized by the Bavarian government during raids in 1786 and published to discredit the group</term> in Bavaria, and have read through it with all attention. I cannot deny that I was very eager for it, but now I know where I stand and what I should think of it. — At first glance, one might believe that the Illuminati would suffer great harm through the **publication** <term>Bekanntmachung: specifically the official government printing of the seized documents, intended to expose the Order as subversive</term> of these writings, just as there are indeed some very striking passages contained therein which I, like you, do not know well how to explain, and which would suggest much malice and danger hidden behind these people. But, since I have the good fortune to know some of them personally and to enjoy their company, I have discussed it with them on this occasion, and they have **lit a light for me** <note>A clever play on words; the name "Illuminati" comes from the Latin *illuminare*, meaning "to enlighten."</note> with which I can now view the matter from the right **point of view**.

<summary>In this response, the author acknowledges receiving the published secret documents of the Illuminati and admits they appear damaging at first, but claims that personal contact with members has provided a more favorable perspective.</summary>
<keywords>Order of the Illuminati, Bavaria, Original Writings, publication, defense, Enlightenment, personal testimony</keywords>

1. First and foremost, one must consider that the papers made known in this writing are primarily nothing but private papers, plans, and essays that never came to fruition, in which, furthermore, not everything is fully expressed; rather, several things are omitted or merely indicated with dashes. Thus, even if offensive sentences truly are found here or there, it is not thereby proven that these were actually brought to maturity, carried out, or disseminated as a whole. Where is the person through whose head some strange thoughts do not pass here or there, which he would be shy to reveal to the world—and where is he who would like to see his entire correspondence printed and placed so naked and bare before the eyes of the public?

2. # Third Letter.

## Reply to the previous one.

From your last letter, my friend, I have seen how you view the matter regarding the published original writings of the Order of the Illuminati <term>Illuminatenorden: A secret society founded in 1776 by Adam Weishaupt in Bavaria, which aimed to promote Enlightenment ideals but was suppressed by the government as a subversive threat</term>, and for what reasons you believe that the society was nothing other than an institution beneficial for the common good and for the promotion of the Enlightenment <term>Aufklärung: The 18th-century intellectual movement that emphasized reason, logic, and individual freedom over tradition and religious dogma</term>. — Precisely this was what first made me particularly attentive. I read once more, deciphered, pondered, and compared, and I must confess that more and more subjects and more and more doubts arise within me, which lead me not only to not expect the so highly praised benefit from the Illuminati society, but rather make me fear much from it.

I want to properly reveal all my reservations to you.

->§. 1.<-

Above all, it is evident from the letters what extraordinary effort the Order took to recruit new followers and to expand. There are specific instructions there for those who

And all this was to be pursued with a courage, with a caution, and yet at the same time with a zeal by each and every person, even by the least significant members of the society <note>original: "Gesellschaft"</note>, who themselves did not yet know where they stood <note>This refers to the "Minervals," the lower ranks of the Illuminati who were kept in the dark about the organization's higher secrets.</note>; it seems as if the happiness <note>original: "Glückseligkeit"</note> of their lives and all their prospects for the future consisted solely in recruiting new members. The choices they were accustomed to observing during this recruitment <term>Recrutierung</term> <note>The systematic process of scouting and initiating new members into the Order.</note> of theirs were, and remain, no less remarkable. The person to be admitted had to possess at least some special advantages through which he could be useful to the Institute <note>The "Institute" refers to the Order of the Illuminati.</note>. Above all, they sought out engaging, enterprising, intriguing, and skillful people; they looked for the noble, the rich, the powerful, and the learned (Page 175). They also looked for "beautiful people," who for the most part possess gentler manners and a more tender heart, and are therefore exceptionally well-suited for negotiations (Page 237). They even required "idiots" <term>Idioten</term> <note>In the 18th-century context, this term often referred to "unlearned" or "simple" people who could be used as tools or "useful innocents" for the Order's lower-level tasks without understanding the higher goals.</note> in greater or lesser numbers depending on the circumstances (Page 125). They had a particular desire for so-called daredevils <note>original: "Teufelskerln," literally "devil-fellows."</note>, who are indeed difficult to direct, but for that very reason are capable of many undertakings and thus very useful (Page 175); and whoever had skillful acquaintances was expected to "deliver" them (Page 268).

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Illuminati's recruitment strategy. The previous page ended with a catchword "a" (einen), leading into the description of how the Order sought to use high-ranking noblemen to attract others.</meta>

( 24 )

...if they only once had a **cavalier** <note>In the 18th century, a "Cavalier" (Kavalier) referred to a gentleman of the court or a man of noble birth and high social standing.</note>, they hoped that he would later deliver the others to them (Page 177.), but it had to be a teachable nobleman not infected with prejudices, where they could dare to let him work with them *with full knowledge of the whole business* <note>original: "sciens de toto negotio"</note>, lend his authority, and also recruit other noblemen (p. 332.). Generally, they sought to force their way into all ranks and offices (p. 390.).

Furthermore, artists were useful to them, primarily painters and **seal-engravers** <term>Petschierstecher</term> <note>Craftsmen who engraved the metal or stone seals used to stamp wax on official correspondence—a vital skill for an organization relying on secret communication.</note>; craftsmen, especially turners, goldsmiths, and locksmiths; also "assistants" <term>Handlanger</term> <note>Literally "hand-longers," referring to those who performed technical or manual tasks for the Order without necessarily being part of the inner philosophical circle.</note>, such as calligraphers and printers, as well as men of protection and reputation. (These are placed immediately after the assistants, and can thus be proud of the esteem held for them by a society to which they lent protection and reputation, sacrificing themselves in the process) (p. 62, 64.) Those who can be of service with book translations and foreign correspondence are likewise counted among the assistants, and booksellers and **postal administrators** <note>Control over the post was crucial for the Illuminati to ensure their secret letters were delivered and to potentially intercept the mail of others.</note> seemed not at all useless to them (p. 199.). Females and monks are indeed excluded on page 54; nonetheless, **Cato** <note>The secret Order-name for Franz Dietrich von Ditfurth (1738–1813), a radical member of the Illuminati who famously pushed for the admission of women into the Order.</note> thought of...

In order to establish this **despotism**, and to strengthen and maintain its reputation and power, the society—if it is to function correctly—must share a single language, the same opinions, the same thoughts, and so forth. What **Spartacus** <note>The code name for Adam Weishaupt (1748–1830), the founder of the Order of the Illuminati</note> wrote was to be carried out exactly as he wrote it, neither more nor less; every week he demanded a report supported by the relevant documents, precise knowledge of all those already admitted and those to be admitted in their full scope, and he insisted that no unauthorized changes be made to the **statutes** <note>The fundamental rules or constitution of the organization</note>. Although this was already a complete despotism, Spartacus did not wish to be viewed as a despot. Since an **aristocracy** <note>In this context, a governing body of multiple high-ranking members sharing power</note> was unthinkable anyway, he reserved all directives for himself, yet claimed to seek and demand nothing but what leads to the goal (p. 347). that looks more similar to the Spanish Inquisition cannot be imagined than the instruction given to the **Superiors** on page 24: to deliver to anyone whose mistakes they have learned of through hearsay—as proof of this—an unsealed, blank sheet containing the word <term>Confiteatur</term> <note>Latin: "Let him confess"; a formal command used to compel a subordinate to admit to a transgression</note>. The offending member is to return this after a certain time, having written down the mistake he imagines he committed. If he hits upon the right one, no further mention is made of the matter; if he does not hit upon it, he receives a second note in which the actual cause is written. — Why did the Order not reveal the mistake to the offender immediately? — Likely because the offender could not know immediately for which specific mistake he was being held accountable. Now, he might well have committed several mistakes. In the belief that the Order had discovered them, he therefore wrote one of these on the **slip of paper** <note>original: *Zedel*</note>, and it was perhaps the very one the Order did not yet know. The subtlety! By which the Order knew how to lure out the most secret thoughts of its fellow brothers in this manner is truly to be admired.

...to remove <note>Continuing from the previous page: "no better opportunity [will be found to] remove..."</note>, as if the matter could be misused; they would never lack for means, and should one or another member rise to prominence, they would always find ways to insinuate themselves <note>original: *insinuiren*; to ingratiate oneself or sneak into a position of influence</note> (p. 390). What, then, can be expected of people who enter into the commonwealth <term>gemeine Wesen: the public sphere, the state, or the community at large</term> with such self-confidence, seeking to shame straightforward nature <note>original: "gerade Natur," referring to an honest, unpretentious, and transparent character</note> with their proud, mysterious, and sage-like demeanor <note>original: "altthuenden Wesen," literally "acting-old," referring to young men affecting the gravity and mystery of ancient sages</note>? Who is held accountable for this inflicted wound and this wasted lifeblood? —

### §. 4.

This alone was bound to produce an extraordinary corruption among many within this Order: that, according to the statutes of the society, they were required to make it their special occupation to observe and investigate others. Every initiate <term>Aufzunehmende: literally "one to be taken in," referring to a candidate or novice in the probationary stage</term> was required to spend his period of trial on the investigation of himself and his fellow human beings <term>Nebenmenschen: those living alongside us; peers or neighbors</term>, and to provide a regular account of these findings. This was like a confession on his own part, and through it, he revealed to the Order all the weak points by which he could be reached <note>original: "beyzukommen war," meaning how he could be influenced, manipulated, or managed</note>; and thus, the foundation was laid for what was eventually to become his trade: spying <note>original: "Spionerie," a term emphasizing the deceptive and invasive nature of these mandatory observations</note> on his fellow men.

The Illuminati were encouraged and assigned, as their primary task, to investigate human characters—their origins, underlying reasons, and consequences—and to analyze them further. They were to examine the grounds and driving forces <term>Triebfedern: literally "mainsprings," a common Enlightenment-era metaphor for the psychological mechanisms that trigger human action</term> behind human actions, to practice the art of characterization and the study of human inclinations, and to learn how such inclinations might be guided, awakened, or destroyed, and so forth (see pages 14 and 15). Through this occupation, they were intended to [sharpen] their attention and their deci-

### §. 4.

What, then, can be expected from people who enter into the commonwealth <term>gemeine Wesen: the public sphere, state affairs, or the "common good"</term> with such self-assurance, who seek to put straightforward nature to shame with their proud, mysterious, and affectedly ancient demeanor? They view the positions to which they are appointed merely as a secure place of rest from which they can better work for their society <note>Referring here to the Order of the Illuminati rather than society at large</note>, and more effectively carry out what they are commanded dictatorially <term>diktatorisch: orders issued from the "Unknown Superiors" of the Order that required absolute obedience without debate</term> from above. They themselves do not know what they are being used for, nor do they perceive the sense or the ultimate purpose <term>Endzweck: the final goal or teleological aim</term> of these commands.

Who is held accountable <term>Rechenschaft: a formal reckoning or responsibility</term> for so many young, hopeful people whose talents <term>Fähigkeiten: innate abilities and potential</term> have now all been given a false direction? It will not be easy for them to work their way back into a healthy track suitable for the commonwealth. Now shamed and misled, they do not immediately know where to turn; having had the potential to become the most useful and productive people, they are now internally disgruntled <note>original: "verstimmt," a musical metaphor suggesting they are like an instrument that has been rendered discordant or "out of tune" with society</note> and lost. Who is held accountable for this inflicted wound, and for this wasted lifeblood? —

### §. 4.

This fact alone was bound to produce an extraordinary corruption <term>Verderbniß: moral decay or depravity</term> among many in this Order: according to the statutes of the society, they were required to make it their primary occupation to observe and investigate others. Every recruit was forced to spend his probationary period researching himself and his fellow man, and to provide a regular accounting <term>Rechenschaft: a formal report or reckoning</term> of these findings. This was like a confession on his part; through it, he revealed to the Order all of his own weaknesses—the very points by which he could be manipulated. Furthermore, the foundation was laid here for what was to eventually become his profession: the espionage <term>Spionerie: systemic spying or surveillance</term> of his fellow human beings.

The Illuminati were advised and tasked, as part of their duties, to research human characters—their origins, motives, and consequences—and to analyze them further. They were to investigate the reasons and driving forces behind human actions, practicing the art of characterization <term>Karakteristik: the psychological profiling and analysis of personality traits</term> and the exploration of human inclinations, as well as how such things are to be guided, awakened, and destroyed, and so forth (see pages 14 and 15) <note>These page numbers likely refer to the "Original Writings" (Originalschriften) of the Illuminati, which were seized and published by the Bavarian Elector in 1787</note>. Through this occupation, they were meant to sharpen their attention and their decision-making <note>The German text ends mid-word with "Entschei-", likely "Entscheidung" or "decision-making."</note>

<summary>This page criticizes the Illuminati's internal statutes, which required members to engage in mutual surveillance and psychological profiling. The author argues that this focus on "espionage" and "characterization" corrupted the members by turning human relationships into a series of tactical investigations.</summary>
<keywords>Order of the Illuminati, surveillance, espionage, psychological profiling, moral corruption, statutes, characterization</keywords>

[...power of] judgment, to practice and strengthen it, in order to go about their work with utility and success. Every <term>Recipiente: an initiator or mentor responsible for a new recruit</term> should therefore ensure he wins the trust of the one he has taken in, to learn of secret relationships through him, and to have character profiles of various individuals drafted (p. 56).

The members were to keep a personal book to record everything good and bad about the people with whom they associated. From those who possessed the <term>facultatem insinuandi: the "faculty of insinuation," or the art of winning people over and gaining their secrets</term>, the society expected their total confidence. Whoever held this rank was required to send in a special sheet under the heading *au Premier* <note>French for "to the first," likely indicating a report for the highest level of leadership</note> in the <term>Ordensschrift: the Order’s secret cipher</term>; this document was to contain all secret intrigues, love affairs, and friendships of various persons (p. 59). Every person was to be recorded according to their name, physique, hair, complexion, gait, and voice, alongside whatever was daily observed about them. However, these notes were to be recorded not by defining the character in general terms, but by the specific deeds from which the character is to be inferred. <note>original: "V. g." for the Latin "Verbi gratia," meaning "for example"</note> For instance, one does not write, "*Titius* <note>a traditional placeholder name in Latin texts, similar to "John Doe"</note> is suspicious," but rather a deed from which suspicion is inferred. [would want done] to himself. How, then, can a good heart be maintained in a person whose occupation is to investigate others according to their good and bad sides, and to provide a detailed report thereof to a <term>society: the Order of the Illuminati</term>, whose <term>superiors: "Obern," the hidden high-ranking leaders of the secret society</term> and <term>driving forces: "Triebfedern," literally the mainsprings of a clock, referring to the hidden motives or mechanisms of the order</term> one does not even know?

Who would willingly suffer this themselves, and what right-thinking person would do this to another? — Such a person would cover the weaknesses of his <term>fellow man: "Nebenmensch," meaning neighbor or peer</term> with a cloak <note>likely a reference to the "cloak of charity," a common historical idiom for covering or forgiving the faults of others</note>; he would not <term>spy on them: "ausspioniren"</term>, record them, and place them in hands where he does not yet know how they will use this information.

— No one can be blamed for wanting to get to know the people with whom one associates, and for placing oneself in a proper state of attentiveness and security against them. No one can be blamed for warning a friend if one sees him taking an incautious step toward someone. But all this is not the case here, where one reveals light to the superiors of the society regarding all secret <term>private matters: "Privatverhältnisse," or personal circumstances</term>, and gives them the opportunity to make the worst use of them, and to carry out everything they please with these weapons in hand.

<summary>The author argues that the Illuminati's practice of secret reporting is incompatible with a "good heart," as it forces members to betray the privacy of their peers to unknown superiors. He contrasts legitimate social caution and friendly warnings with the systematic collection of private "weapons" used by the Order's leadership.</summary>

are strangers. Therefore, most of the **curious** are foolish and unrestrained. The more they know secrets are kept from them, the more impetuously they seek to discover them. Marius <note>Marius was the Illuminati pseudonym for Jakob Anton von Hertel (1735–1792), a counselor in Munich who had access to official collections</note> was to share with them whatever he had from it, and not make a "case of conscience" <note>original: *Casum conscientiae*; a theological or moral dilemma regarding whether an action is a sin</note> out of it; for only that which causes harm is sin, and when the benefit becomes greater than the harm, it even becomes a virtue. In their hands, the books would certainly be of more use than if they were locked up in their original locations for a hundred years. (p. 242, 330.)

Their love for literature went so far that there was even a proposal that every superior should be able to write with both hands (p. 107), presumably to be able to contribute even more to the general good. — To collect unpublished works <note>original: *Inedita*; manuscripts or ancient texts that have not yet been printed</note>...

<meta>This page continues the ethical debate regarding the Illuminati’s methods of acquiring knowledge. Meanwhile, they are all said to have possessed a fully developed literature; — does this permit the theft of these libraries? — Modest use of them is not difficult to obtain anyway; why then steal from them afterwards, and by what right? —— It is, after all, a good and a piece of property, just like silver table service <term>Tafelzeug: a set of fine silverware and linens for formal dining, often kept as a family heirloom</term>, which a wealthy merchant might have lying unused in a chest for twenty years. The owner does not use it either. And if I am allowed to steal books that the owner does not use, then I may also misappropriate the silver table service because the owner does not use it. —— And has it even been proven that these books lie unused in the libraries? — He who acquired and collected them must surely have possessed insight and been of a noble mind to contribute so much to the enlightenment <term>Aufklärung: the 18th-century intellectual movement (the Enlightenment) emphasizing reason and the spread of knowledge</term> of his descendants. And if dust lies upon them for five years, perhaps one of the rightful owners will find it in the sixth year and rejoice in the treasure he has found, or serve a good friend with it. — How abominable it is, moreover, to withhold entrusted items, will, I hope, not need to be proven first.

->( 75 )<-

If one is permitted to take what the owner simply does not know how to use, and if the maxim <term>Satz: a philosophical principle or proposition</term> holds that only that which causes harm is a sin, and if the benefit becomes greater than the harm, such that the harm even becomes a virtue; if this principle holds, then it would be no feat at all to shortly bring about a general war and universal confusion across the globe. — People think differently according to their own advantage, and everyone would soon form their own private definitions of what brings benefit and harm; in doing so, one would not be concerned with the benefit or harm of the commonwealth <term>gemeinen Wesen: the public good, or society as a collective whole</term>, but would always be intent only on one's own benefit and harm. Furthermore, there is no evil deed in the world from which one could not, if one wished, rationalize <term>herausklügeln: to use overly subtle, "clever," or sophistical reasoning to justify something</term> some specific benefit. — The outbursts of all passions <term>Leidenschaften: in 18th-century thought, these were powerful emotional drives (like anger or lust) that were often contrasted with rational self-control</term> could be excused; for if a person did not have this or that passion, they would also not possess this or that good quality; and to send a man out of the world with one's own hand <note>a euphemism for assassination or murder</note>—someone whom one believes to be harmful to the commonwealth or to oneself—could then be considered a meritorious work. — If this principle holds, then all peace treaties

Special respect, fame, and honor—which are truly due only to upright men of excellent merit—were thus granted by the society only according to the measure of how far someone was capable of providing utility to the society; and nothing more was required than to be admitted into the Order and to view all its fogs <note>The author uses "fogs" (Nebel) metaphorically to describe the Order's obscure or deceptive practices, which members were expected to praise as if they were holy "incense."</note> as fragrant incense, in order to be praised in all places. From this it is clear that the promotion of worthy persons was only considered insofar as they were in the Order and could be useful to the Order in one way or another.

<meta>catchword: one,</meta>

One could hardly attain promotion, fame, and honor in a more convenient and certain way than this. Another person must often work for years until he finds someone who takes an interest in him with special zeal and warmth, and until he distinguishes himself among his fellow citizens in one way or another to his own advantage; and all of this remains merely accidental, and there is no collective consensus that speaks for him. Here, however, *by profession* <note>original: "ex professo"; meaning deliberately or as a matter of their stated purpose.</note> everything aligns on every occasion to his praise, and in a short time he is portrayed as such a man that a profane person <note>A "profane person" (Profaner) refers to someone outside the secret society; an uninitiated outsider.</note> is not worthy to hand him water or to loosen his shoestrings. And where one of these gentlemen sat at the helm, or had something to bestow, and two people applied for a position—one of whom was a Brother and one was an outsider—this, according to the statutes of the Order, permitted no further delay, and the Brother had to be promoted before the outsider. — It appears, then, that these gentlemen belonged not to the state and their office, but solely to their Order; and to the state and the office only insofar as there was something to be done therein for the benefit of the Order. One should have reflected upon the fact that the accusations against the <term>Jesuits</term> <note>The Society of Jesus, often the primary rival and model for the Illuminati.</note>—concerning their power and their intrigues—were applicable to no one so much as to the <term>Illuminati</term> themselves, and that <term>Cato’s</term> <note>Likely a reference to Cato the Elder, known in Roman history for his strict morality and his warnings against the corruption of the state.</note> warning regarding slander and immoderate accusations might not have been most strictly observed here. — It was therefore left purely to the <term>arbitrary will</term> <note>original: "Willkühr"; implying a lack of legal oversight or objective rules.</note> of the society to dictate how one should behave toward the enemies of the Order. The society thus viewed itself internally as a legally established society and took up its own **sword of vengeance** against transgressors. Marius <note>The secret code name for Baron von Hertel</note> was to be spared entirely from religious intentions, as his stomach was not yet fully prepared to digest such strong food (p. 223): and Cato <note>The code name for Xavier von Zwack (1755–1843), a legal counselor and one of the most important early members of the Illuminati</note> believed that it would be less harmful to a society to have members who do not believe in any God at all than to tolerate some who imagine a God who is angry, vengeful, and endowed with human passions or perfections. Atheism, he argued, finds its pleasure and its only happiness in social life; it applies everything toward being happy within that life and benefiting others through its merits. Even if the underlying reason is merely self-love seeking to elevate itself, some advantage still always accrues to the whole. Superstition, by contrast, teaches one to despise the world and to make it despicable to others; it views everything charming that the world promises to those who serve it—and with which it encourages everyone—as seductive and harmful. They sought, therefore, to be "reformers in silence" <note>original: *Reformateurs im Stillen*</note>, and to forge anew and change the entire religious system in another way, according to their own concepts and as their advantage demanded. It must not have been merely a matter of **rational ideas**; otherwise, they would not have regarded them as such "strong meat" <note>original: *starke Speise*; a biblical metaphor (Hebrews 5:12) referring to advanced or difficult doctrines that "weaker" believers are not yet ready to "digest"</note> that one stomach or another would be unable to tolerate it. While it is indeed fair and consistent with sound reason to work against superstition in every conceivable way, the utmost caution is nevertheless required. For this very reason—because the knowledge of **natural religion** <term>natural religion: a system of belief based on reason and the observation of the natural world, rather than on divine revelation or established church dogma</term> and the understanding of the truths for living that flow from it require so much study, which cannot be expected of the farmer or the craftsman, nor even of many who call themselves scholars,

### §. 10.

Just as Illuminatism <note>The Illuminati was a secret society founded in 1776 by Adam Weishaupt in Bavaria; it became a symbol of radical Enlightenment thought that many contemporaries feared would subvert Church and State.</note> intended to undertake a reformation in religion, it wanted to do the same in morality, and to let a very peculiar system of its own emerge in a short time.

The ultimate purpose of the Order, it was said, was that "there be light," <note>original: "daß es Licht werde"; a reference to the Creation in Genesis, here used by the Order to symbolize the "light" of reason or enlightenment.</note> and that they were warriors against darkness (p. 331). They wanted to guide the intellect and improve the heart, and to assist oppressed virtue against injustice; as if they were pure virtue themselves and committed no injustice (p. 38). Cato <note>Cato was the secret "Order name" of Xavier Zwack (1755–1843), a prominent lawyer and central figure in the Bavarian Illuminati.</note> promised (p. 81) never to join evil, self-serving, or despotic intentions, and to apply himself to the greatest impartiality. — And yet there were no intentions more despotic than those of the Society, and how could those apply themselves to impartiality who had to promise never to use their status or their office against their fellow brothers of the Order?

They wanted (as they said) to alert the prince and the citizen to abuses, and yet they themselves had their own ceremonies and customs. They wanted to combat prejudices <note>original: "Vorurtheile"; in this era, the word often referred to established social traditions and religious dogmas that Enlightenment thinkers viewed as irrational.</note>, and yet they feigned a presumptuous moral regime <note>original: "Sittenregiment"; a term suggesting a strict, often intrusive control over the private behavior and ethics of others.</note>, and a pre-

### §. 11.

The transformation that the <term>Illuminati (A secret society founded in Bavaria in 1776 by Adam Weishaupt, aiming to oppose religious influence and state abuse of power)</term> planned for religion and morality was also looming for the state constitution itself. This was the case even though they gave assurances that nothing would be undertaken against the state, religion, or morality (p. 60), and despite their attempts to mislead people into believing they could be of greater service within the society than they would be capable of individually (p. 232). They state themselves that the initiate assumes unknown obligations (p. 68)—as if one did not already have enough obligations which one is scarcely able to fulfill. And when it is remarked that things are only now becoming truly important, and that caution and prudence are necessary to the highest degree, then—

The Order therefore took precedence, and **state duties** were regarded only as incidental. Anyone who can speak of not wanting to dissolve the rational bonds of the state must already presume a special power for himself—as if it depended on him whether to dissolve these bonds or not, and as if it depended on his signal whether he wished to grant land and soil to arriving foreigners or not.

It was said, to be sure, that everyone should preside over his **family** as a good father, husband, son, master, and servant; but how was that possible when, at the same time, one person was made a **spy** for another, and according to the rules of the **institute**, one was required to discover and betray the weaknesses of the other? In this way, they sought to exert a certain supreme authority over all citizens, whether they were incorporated into the Order or not.

Since they drew all manner of **foreigners** into this institute and sought to spread themselves abroad as much as possible, what value could **patriotism** <note>original: "Vaterlandsliebe"; literally "love of the fatherland," a key 18th-century virtue</note> have within the Order?

And how sad when the **profane** <note>original: "Profane"; a term used by secret societies to describe those who have not been initiated</note> were prevented by the <term>Order</term> <note>Order: Here referring to the Illuminati, whom the author accuses of gatekeeping professional success</note> from receiving a salary or a bonus for their sweat and labor, while the **brothers of the order** <note>original: "Ordensbrüdern"</note> lacked for nothing and their will was done in everything.

There are various funds <term>Kassen: financial treasuries or accounts held by organizations or charitable trusts</term>, established and founded through endowments or through proven and authorized societies, all of which are utilized and managed according to a certain plan aimed at the general welfare. Es <note>This "Es" is a signature mark at the bottom of the previous page, intended to help the binder or reader transition to the next page.</note>

There is a class of people whose **character** is entirely contrary to the rest of civilized society. Initially, they seek only to draw the observation of others upon themselves through their peculiar behavior; they wish to elevate themselves through their heedlessness, and, in their own opinion, count themselves among the sect of the **Stoics**. <term>Stoics: a reference to the ancient Greek school of philosophy that taught self-control and detachment from emotions; here, the author implies these individuals use it as a mask for antisocial behavior.</term> Eventually, they reach the point where, if the inclination toward social life were not so universal and the needs of life so pressing, they would never deign to live under laws with certain contracts of philanthropy, social propriety, and high esteem. Their unrestrained and offensive nature draws upon them the general contempt of their fellow creatures; they are never honored with distinctions, they remain in the lowest class of society, and they are regarded as a useless, burdensome load from which one wishes to be liberated. These people, as one can imagine, are inclined toward nothing more than the creation of confusion; dissatisfied with themselves, without indulgence for the transgressions of others, inflexible, rude, defiant, and miserable slaves of their **fanaticism** <term>fanaticism: translated from the German "Schwärmerey," a common 18th-century term for irrational enthusiasm or visionary dreaming, often used to criticize secret societies</term>, they are led to actions which are nothing but insults against their fellow brothers. ———

[the] faithful image of the true <term>closet scholars: translated from "Stubengelehrten," referring to academics who study in isolation and lack practical experience of the world.</term>, or so-called philosophers, who believe that at their desks and within their narrow rooms, they can design plans for the mastery of the whole world. They neither receive nor pay visits, and if they should happen to attend public amusements or banquets, they remain detached and indifferent. They smile condescendingly at the common crowd that finds joy in such things, wishing to appear far cleverer, and they speak of matters entirely foreign to the social circle; for example, of state affairs, of the East Indies, of voyages around the world, and the like. 

On their walks, they carry large books with them, going either entirely alone or only with those by whom they know they are worshipped. Wherever they go, they find nothing in its proper place, and they always find something to criticize. In secret, they subsequently incite people against one another; they are lustful, greedy, and ambitious to the highest degree. They claim they do not care for the <term>applause: translated from "Beyfall," meaning public approval or acclaim.</term> of the common crowd, but their bile boils when it eludes them, and in their "black blood" <note>This is a reference to the classical medical theory of the four humors, where "black bile" was associated with a melancholy, bitter, or cynical temperament.</note> they nourish thoughts of discord and persecution. Yet they always consider themselves cleverer than other people and smile down at the common crowd.

These individuals often deliberately stir up discords so that they might exploit them for their own benefit. Among this group belong primarily those whose dominant passion is <term>pride: translated from "Stolz," implying an arrogant or overbearing sense of self-importance.</term>.

To satisfy this inclination and to indulge it with as little interference as possible, they seek to persuade those who are aware of their passion of just how much utility and sublimity can be expected from such noble sentiments; glittering examples must then be brought forward to confirm their thesis. Next, they strive to obtain security against all insults from <term>authorities: translated from "Obrigkeiten," referring to the governing bodies, magistrates, or the established legal ruling class.</term> and other members. Since fairness must necessarily permit this, they employ various seemingly honest means to bring it about. 

They procure friends for themselves and acquire a following for the sake of their "just cause," which they increase either with the help of their money, or through their protection and prestige, or through their engaging and polite social manner. Similar consequences are to be feared from those who are governed by <term>private hatred: translated from "Privathaß," meaning personal animosity or grudges held outside of the public interest.</term> and <term>self-revenge: translated from "Selbstrache," the act of taking the law into one's own hands rather than seeking legal redress.</term>. What can one expect from the conduct of someone who wishes to be his own judge for every perceived insult? What of the man who is never satisfied with any restitution, and who—to satiate his personal hatred—demands that even the most honorable man, upon whom the prosperity of society depends, be sacrificed to his fury? How can one hope for calm and peaceful behavior from those who nourish the seeds of war and unrest in their hearts? ——

<meta>Continuity from page 121. This does not, however, perfectly agree with the <term>ultimate goal<note>Original: "Endzwecke." This refers back to the Illuminati's claim that their final purpose was the moral perfection of humanity.</note></term> stated above by the Order. — Why the silent and hidden power, and the insight into the most hidden secrets? — Anyone who wants to do good finds enough opportunity to do so in their <term>professional duties<note>Original: "Berufsgeschäften." The fact alone that the Order states on page 39: that which is so beautifully described is its sole and unembellished <note>original: "nicht colorierte" — meaning straightforward or without hidden agendas</note> ultimate goal, proves precisely that it *was* the embellished <note>original: "colorierte" — implying a facade or a hidden coloring of the truth</note> goal. II. That I can lose all else, and more besides, through my fellow men, just as I received it through them.

Can everything then be received and lost through fellow men? — Even virtue? — Even the consciousness of having acted uprightly? — A man who claims to be able to receive and lose everything through his fellow men usually has no confidence in his own strengths; instead, he relies merely on the circumstances in which he stands, on the many connections he has formed, and on the hope of being trumpeted about by his fellow brothers <note>original: "Mitbrüdern," referring to lodge members or associates.</note>, and if he only knows the art of making himself obliging and pleasing to many people, then his fortune is made. — — And how easy that is in current times, where the trifling tone <note>original: "tändelnde Ton"; this refers to a superficial, frivolous, or overly "gallant" style of social interaction prevalent in 18th-century salons.</note> has become so general, and where one never asks of a man: Is he equal to the task? — does he understand what

The **Inquisition in Spain** does not follow such shameful principles, and an open rebellion would be less harmful than this <term>state within a state</term><note>A political situation where a secret society or organization operates its own law and authority independently of the country's actual government.</note>, and this **invisible poison**. VII. I pledge to regard the best interests of the Order as my own, and am prepared, as long as I am a member thereof, to serve it with my property, honor, and life-blood.

The state, when it admits a new citizen, and every council assembly that works for the benefit of the Fatherland, can demand no greater or more important obligation from its candidates than this one. — He who serves with property and blood does what he is capable of doing. ### §. 14.

If one considers all of this carefully, my friend! then it will likely not be difficult to answer the twelve concerns you have addressed to me.

1. To be sure, many of the original writings consist merely of letters and private papers. However, these are nothing but letters exchanged by the <term>Consciis: From the Latin, meaning those "in the know" or co-conspirators who were privy to the secret plans</term> for the advancement and elevation of the society in this specific way and no other. And I believe the true meaning of the matter shines through much more clearly from such letters than from the statutes <note>original: "Instituten," referring to the formal rules or founding documents of the organization</note>, several of which have been printed with various deceptive articles. What appears in the statutes merely as an abstract or general maxim stands here in the letters in its naked nature; one knows what is being said and what the ultimate goal is. Moreover, the brothers were required to contribute all their strength for the benefit of the society and to do everything according to the <term>Intention: The underlying aim or hidden agenda of the Order, which often differed from its public face</term> of the society.

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that the private correspondence of the secret society members is just as important as their official rules. For people such as these, all private papers are significant; for everything is directed toward their **system**, or at least bears its mark. It has certainly not been proven that many of these offensive propositions <note>original: "anstößige Sätze," referring to the radical or subversive ideas found in the society's internal documents</note> never reached maturity or were never put into practice. But even if we assume the case that they did not reach maturity, it is enough that they were even proposed. One would not tolerate someone walking into a hay or straw warehouse with a burning piece of wood, even if they promised a hundred times that they did not intend to set anything on fire. So it is here. The human heart has too many facets and too many moods. If an offensive proposition is raised—even without a specific plan—it is and remains dangerous for the simple reason that one cannot know where it might take root and grow. 

However, that such propositions were raised here not without intent, but *with* intent, is evident on every page. Every person likely has strange thoughts cross their mind from time to time; but anyone who is rational learns better through more precise experience and does not wish to spread their whim or turn it into a law. What is found in these **original writings** <note>original: "Originalschriften," a term often used in the 18th century to refer to the seized documents of the Illuminati</note>, however, they intended to make universal

3. In this manner, and given what has been stated above, I cannot comprehend how these offensive <note>original: "anstößigen." These are the controversial or "objectionable" passages in the secret writings of the Illuminati that were discovered and published by the Bavarian government.</note> passages could possibly be interpreted in a favorable light. Why would you choose to assume the best about them? It is true that we are obliged to think the best of every one of our fellow men <term>Nebenmenschen: literally "neighbor-humans"; our fellow human beings</term>. Yet, when one perceives ambiguous matters in a neighbor that cause concern for one's own safety, one cannot be blamed for locking the front door and remaining somewhat on guard.

The same applies here. These offensive passages are of such a nature that—even if they were merely thoughts without actions—they must, in and of themselves, arouse mistrust toward the people who harbor such thoughts. It is impossible for truly good sentiments <term>Gesinnungen: a person's inner moral character, outlook, or set of principles</term> to find a place or flourish alongside such ideas. The weeds rob the sprouting good seed of its nourishment, cover it with their foliage, deprive it of the sun’s warmth, and thus suffocate it in its earliest days.

Furthermore, within the society <note>Referring here to the Order of the Illuminati.</note>, everything was meant to be pursued with the greatest zeal from the top down. And anyone who wished to advance further and gain deeper insights would certainly not have failed to do so. Rather, one hitches up large trees so that **clay** <term>Letten; a type of heavy, fatty loam or clay used in 18th-century hydraulic engineering to reinforce banks and catch sediment</term> and similar materials might cling to them, thereby nudging the river sideways of its own accord. The Illuminati are always complaining about intolerance, and that people do not have enough forbearance <note>original: "Duldung" - meaning tolerance or legal recognition</note> for them, — — — Yet they themselves are the first to spy everywhere on all secrets, seeking to fill all positions with their brothers, to displace all the profane <term>Profanen: those outside the secret society; the uninitiated</term> from those offices, to unseat <note>original: "aus dem Sattel zu heben" - literally "to lift out of the saddle," an idiom for toppling someone from their position or rank</note> all those who stand in their way, and whenever it is at all feasible, to set upon them with libelous writings. Is this tolerance? — And are they themselves not the most intolerant of people? — It is incomprehensible how the Order can demand so many things from others that it neither possesses nor practices itself. It is incomprehensible to demand that the State should watch a "new state"—which seeks to raise its head above the others—emerge in its midst as one of the most dangerous and widespread systems, without being moved by it; that the State should slumber so that the new system might reach its height all the more securely, thereby earning itself the grand name of "tolerance." One does not contest Enlightenment <term>Aufklärung: the 18th-century intellectual movement emphasizing reason over tradition</term> and good intentions; but one contests a system that only wears Enlightenment and good intentions as a mask in order to attain its secret power all the more certainly, and to direct everything according to its own will. And this is truly not intolerance, but—

Under these circumstances, it is proving more and more that the Order did not consider itself bound to give the State an account of its existence and its constitution <note>original: "Verfassung"; here referring to the group's internal laws and organizational structure rather than a national constitution</note>, and consequently did not consider itself bound to obey the government. In doing so, they wanted to have a hand in everything that happened, and therefore strove for nothing else than to tear themselves away from all submission to an orderly form of government, to take the general helm into their own hands, to establish a state of their own, and to rule themselves—and even after their suppression <note>This likely refers to the 1785 ban on the Illuminati by the Bavarian Elector Charles Theodore</note>, never giving up the hope of raising their heads all the more securely.

But in precisely these secret societies, one finds so many power-hungry, self-infatuated egotists who, with unlimited confidence in their own excellence and an extraordinary pride in their secret <term>nexus</term> <note>original: *Nexus*; a Latin-derived term referring to the internal network, bond, or "connection" shared by members of a secret group.</note>, consider themselves better than others and disturb the peace of the ordinary society of the state. There is no better brotherly equality than when everyone does their duty in their own field, making themselves respectable to their superiors, and thereby drawing down their trust and esteem from above. Even without a secret society, every upright man finds enough opportunity among good friends in a happy hour to enjoy his life without an <term>apron</term> <note>original: "Schurzfell" — a piece of ritual clothing worn by members of Freemasonry and related secret societies.</note> and <term>working tools</term> <note>original: "Arbeitszeug" — symbolic implements used in ritual work, such as the square and compass.</note>, without memorized sayings and ceremonies; and the freer and more unconstrained these occasions are, the more pleasant they must also be. — The original writings prove that the secret of the <term>Illuminati</term> <note>A secret society founded in Bavaria in 1776 by Adam Weishaupt, which sought to promote Enlightenment ideals but was suppressed by the government.</note> contained much that was contrary to higher duties and detrimental to individual people. Since the members of the order were, above all, citizens of the state, it was also their duty to disclose their knowledge of the matter to the state upon its request. Yet the order considers this a crime, claiming it is improper to betray one's brothers and abuse their trust. — Very strange. The order did not consider it a crime for its brothers to spy on their parents, relatives, superiors, and friends, betraying them to the order, and thus abusing the most sacred trust.

<meta>This page continues the critique of secret societies, particularly the Illuminati, moving from the personal betrayal of trust discussed on the previous page to the broader political and legal threat they pose to the state. ( 199 )

From all of this it follows that it cannot be permitted for such <term>secret associations</term> <note>original: "geheime Verbindungen"</note> to acquire such power. The <term>Original Writings</term> <note>original: "Originalschriften" — This refers to the actual internal documents and correspondence of the Illuminati that were seized during government raids on members' homes (such as Xavier von Zwack's) in 1786 and 1787.</note> prove that they neither acquired this power lawfully nor used it lawfully; that it was obtained by stealth, usurped, and illegal. And if the <term>Illuminati</term> <note>A secret society founded in 1776 by Adam Weishaupt in Bavaria, which aimed to replace traditional religion and state structures with Enlightenment rationalism.</note> intended to bring our <term>fatherland</term> <note>original: "Vaterland" — Here referring to the Electorate of Bavaria.</note> under their control in a short time, then it would be very wise to chop down the growing tree that was intended to provide the beams for this new Illuminati government over our fatherland.

The Original Writings prove that <term>Illuminatism</term> <note>original: "Illuminatismus" — The specific system, ideology, and organizational methods of the Order.</note> has abused its power. This has been discussed, and one therefore never needs to ask again whether a secret society is capable of abusing its power. Since neither their purpose, organization, methods, nor members are known, no one can vouch for their integrity. Through the Original Writings, it has been revealed that several of the leading members were not entirely beyond reproach in their actions, and that <term>civic duties</term> <note>original: "bürgerliche Pflichten" — The legal and moral obligations a citizen owes to their state and sovereign.</note> were only to be followed insofar as they did not hinder one's duties to the Illuminati. This is always dangerous enough, and one may therefore rightly question such matters.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the dangers posed by secret societies, specifically referencing the history and documented evidence regarding the Illuminati. It focuses on the threat their secrecy poses to the State.</meta>

It is and remains certain, as the *History of the Illuminati* <note>original: "Geſchichte der Illuminaten"</note> states on page 41: A number of significant people have joined together in secret; the powers of the individuals become the powers of all. No one knows their intentions, no one sees the course of their affairs. Under the cloak of darkness, they can venture anything. To resist their attacks is a pure impossibility, because one cannot see them. Even assuming that their primary purpose were good and tolerable, they are humans. They bring their passions into the league. They can abuse their united strength to satisfy these passions. Their superiors and leaders can use the devotion of the others for their evil or selfish intentions. What are such people not capable of doing? — What is there not to fear from them? — How true all this is, is shown by the <term>original writings</term> <note>This likely refers to the "Einige Originalschriften des Illuminatenordens," a collection of seized documents published by the Bavarian government in 1787 to expose the group's internal workings.</note> in greater detail. — Even if the Order, under these circumstances and if it really could produce so much evil, could not have remained unnoticed for long; is the State bound to wait so long — or must it, as soon as it sees the danger, intervene? — Their internal power is great enough to carry out various intentions; they have indeed proven that. —

The secret societies live in a state of constant war even among themselves, and each faction hopes that it will expand and strengthen all the more through the decrees issued against it. They always strive to make everything outside of their own circle appear ridiculous or contemptible in order to elevate themselves further; and these traits alone should be enough to inspire mistrust against all such secret associations. Yet they always wish to enjoy an undisturbed, silent tolerance, and no one is supposed to inquire into what they are doing.

And if the Order sought to bring the entire fatherland under its power, it surely had its own motives for doing so. Whether these were good or evil motives may be decided by the other sentiments that the Order generally revealed.

The Order claims on page 83 that the persecution of the Order was caused, in part, by the breached silence of some of its members. However, it is well known that it is not quite possible to maintain such an extraordinary silence among so many people. If the intention of the Order is thoroughly good and for the benefit of the <term>commonwealth</term> <note>original: "gemeinen Wesen." This term refers to the collective body of the state and the public good.</note>, then it should also strive to stand firm on its own feet for the sake of that very common good. If secrecy, which is so easily compromised, stands in its way, let it throw itself into the arms of the state and seek security and support there! The extraordinary mystery and silence would then fall away, and the benevolent goals one has in mind could then be carried out all the more securely.

that so many disruptions arose from this
society. Amidst this diversity
of interests and opinions, each of which had to be
defended with heat and conviction by
its followers, the member of the Order <term>Ordensbruder: literally "brother of the order," referring here to an initiate of the Illuminati</term>
forgot his office and other duties, and sought
only to judge and unify everything according
to the interest instilled in him by the Order,
and according to the opinions derived therefrom.
His whole life is insatiable in its demands,
few of which are ever satisfied, full of ambitious
projects, intentions, and fruitless, failed,
doubtful, and dangerous attempts. Hence arise
weariness and disgust, hence the inability to
procure for oneself higher and more vivid
pleasures, hence a quiet restlessness; for hap-
piness here on earth requires something more than
secret power and authority, and insight into all
family affairs <note>This refers to the Order's practice of "Quibus Licet" reports, where members were expected to spy on and report the private conduct of their peers and families to their superiors</note>.

Thus, for that which every in-
stitution <term>Institut: an organized society or establishment</term> is actually supposed to be and serve, there was a lack of the...

<meta>This page concludes the critique of the Illuminati's social and psychological impact. It transitions from the previous page's discussion on the "lack" of true purpose to describe how the Order’s methods destroyed private trust and concludes the author's lengthy epistolary analysis of the secret society.</meta>

<page-num>( 232 )</page-num>

...happiness to be effected for mankind. <term>Apparent happiness: a surface-level or illusory sense of well-being, which the author contrasts with true moral fulfillment</term> attracted the members more intensely. The ambiguous means used to achieve it became the focus of their thoughts and conversations, shaping their mindsets; these means became increasingly familiar to them, gradually merging with their habits, and produced outcomes that are entirely contrary to true happiness. Through constant spying <note>original: "ewige Auflauren." This refers to the Order's "Quibus Licet" system, where members were required to secretively observe and report on the behavior of their peers and family members to their superiors.</note>, there was no longer any freedom at festivities, no confidence among parents, and no trust within families. Friendship truly became a pitfall here. Candor became dangerous, and to criticize anything about this <term>institution: the organized society of the Illuminati</term> was regarded as an attempt to work against the cause of total <term>Enlightenment: the intellectual movement emphasizing reason, which the Order claimed to represent</term> and tolerance.

This was a long letter, my friend! And I have now laid out all my concerns and doubts regarding <term>Illuminatism: the system and principles of the Order of the Illuminati</term> before you. In the last three paragraphs, I have occasionally used the words of the Illuminati themselves where it seemed appropriate. Without wishing to offend anyone personally, these are merely propositions regarding the entire system itself, and regarding the consequences that were bound to result for the heart and mind of the nation.

<summary>This page concludes a critical letter describing how the Illuminati's culture of surveillance destroyed domestic trust and social freedom. The author argues that the Order's system corrupted the "heart and mind of the nation" while hiding behind the labels of Enlightenment and tolerance.</summary>

<keywords>Illuminati, surveillance, domestic trust, Enlightenment, social critique, Illuminatism, moral character</keywords>

--- Page 245 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the public controversy surrounding the Illuminati in Germany. The catchword "Alle" (All) at the bottom suggests the following page begins with a biblical or philosophical quotation, likely from the Book of Ecclesiastes.</meta>

In due time and at your convenience, write to me regarding your concerns about this! Since the matter interests the entire *nation*, since so many agitations <note>original: "Gährungen," literally "fermentations"; refers to civil or intellectual unrest</note> have arisen from it, and since all of Germany has fixed its attention on this story, it is worth the effort to consider, weigh, and bring to light every aspect of it—especially since the matter has finally been discussed in public.

Thus, once a harmful and excessive weight has been gained on one side, great movements must also occur on the other to bring things back onto the right track; and since this is the case here, I believe that of all that has been said, nothing has been said in vain. — It is precisely this weighing of reasons on each side that allows one to clarify and better understand things, so that at last the day of peace and general trust—which has been so greatly hindered by the progress of *Illuminatism* <note>The doctrines or influence of the Order of the Illuminati, a secret society founded in Bavaria in 1776 that became a focal point for conspiracy theories and political anxiety</note>—may shine once again.

Until those better hours of the future, my friend! Until everything is calmed, and until the association of the *Illuminati* fully integrates itself into the course of the public interest <note>original: "gemeinen Wesens," referring to the state or the common good</note>, speak with *Solomon*:

<summary>The author argues that the public controversy surrounding the Illuminati requires a balanced examination of all sides to restore national peace and trust. He looks forward to a future where the secret society is either settled or fully integrated into the common good.</summary>
<keywords>Illuminati, Germany, public debate, social unrest, Solomon, Illuminatism, national interest</keywords>

<meta>This page provides the biblical conclusion to the preceding discussion regarding the Illuminati controversy. All things have their own time, and everything that is under heaven passes away in its time. To be born and to die, — to plant and to harvest, — to strike and to heal, — to break and to build, — to weep and to laugh, — to mourn and to dance, — to gather stones and to cast them away, — to embrace and to push away, to acquire <note>original: "überkommen"; in the context of this biblical verse, it refers to gaining or seeking possessions</note> and to lose, — to keep and to throw away, — to tear and to sew, — to be silent and to speak, — to love and to hate, — conflict and joy <note>original: "Streit und Freude"; while standard English Bibles usually translate this pair as "war and peace," the author's German source uses "conflict and joy"</note>, — everything has its time. Ecclesiastes Chapter 3 <note>original: "Eccl. 3. cap."; the author cites the Book of Ecclesiastes, traditionally attributed to King Solomon, whose "better hours" were mentioned on the previous page</note>.

But I am and remain at all times
